| **[Causal Spatiotemporal State Evolution for Monocular 4D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27594v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While feed-forward models excel in static 3D geometry estimation, extending them to dynamic 4D scenes requires modeling visual geometry as a causally evolving state. Current methods typically rely on unordered temporal aggregation, which lacks the directional structure of physical time. Furthermore, existing supervision often treats geometry and motion as disjoint objectives, failing to exploit their inherent physical coupling. Consequently, they lack a unified constraint to effectively supervise temporal state transitions, leading to inconsistent 4D scene representations. To address this, we propose a feed-forward framework for causal 4D visual geometry learning from monocular videos. At its core, Causal Spatiotemporal Mamba augments temporal aggregation with directional state propagation. Through complementary Temporal-Spatial and Spatial-Temporal Scans, CSM continuously propagates geometry-aware states across frames to model spatiotemporal evolution. To align this causal state evolution with observable dynamics, we introduce Unified Motion Supervision. Rather than treating motion as an independent objective, UMS leverages geometry-aware predictions from the camera and depth heads to construct a unified forward motion field that is consistent with the underlying 3D structure. This geometry-grounded supervision explicitly constrains inter-frame state transitions, enabling stable states propagation across frames. Extensive evaluations on dynamic benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across video depth, camera pose, and optical flow estimation. These results validate that the synergy between the directional state propagation of Causal Spatiotemporal Mamba and Unified Motion Supervision effectively captures continuous scene evolution, significantly reducing temporal drift and reinforcing spatial-temporal consistency in 4D representation learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[STEAM: Stable Self-Training with Elastic Matching and Adaptive Purification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09057v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-view geo-localization (CVGL) aims to perform visual localization by matching drone-view images with corresponding satellite images. To address these limitations, we propose STEAM (Stable Self-Training with Elastic Matching and Adaptive Purification), a direct self-training framework for unsupervised cross-view geo-localization. It combines end-to-end representation learning with iterative pseudo-label refinement without image-generation-based warm-up or clustering. Specifically, the Stable Spatial-Aware Module constructs image descriptors through bounded nonlinear spatial weighting. Adaptive Purification maintains the pseudo-label repository through Confidence-Aware Updates, Age-Aware Updates, and Expired Label Removal. These results indicate that directional mixture-to-constituent alignment provides an effective pre-training signal for polyphonic audio representation learning.</p></details> | 13 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Registration-Grounded Spectral Fusion for Unregistered WLI/NBI Endoscopic Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26395v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>White-light imaging (WLI) and narrow-band imaging (NBI) provide complementary cues for endoscopic lesion segmentation, yet their sequential acquisition often results in spatially inconsistent observations. | **[Representing short-term movement to reveal animal identity and learning progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27140v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How learning progresses over time and varies across individuals is a longstanding question in cognitive and behavioral science. Using behavior data from freely behaving rats, we show that the learned representations not only improve prediction of identity and learning-related readouts over existing methods, but also provide a tool for investigating broader scientific questions about when and how these signals appear in behavior. Specifically, trajectories as short as one second contain informative learning and identity signals. Egocentric pose dynamics are more predictive of identity, whereas allocentric motion is more predictive of learning-related variation, and both signals are strongest at decision junctions in the alternation cohort. We further use a representation-conditioned diffusion model to inspect movement patterns associated with identity and learning progress. D.2</p></details> |

The key idea is that parts/wholes can be modelled as higher-dimensional vectors ($d \geq 4$), and information can be encoded in their relative phase differences. This survey synthesises these developments across invasive and non-invasive measurements. We connect Articulated, Inner and Perceived paradigms to the neural populations they engage, the representations available to decoders and the outputs those representations can support. We examine model development, public resources and the evolution of evaluation, and compare published performance and communication costs within their reported protocols. The synthesis identifies complementary routes to progress: phonetic, acoustic and semantic targets preserve different aspects of a message; shared representations support reuse across recording conditions and tasks; and online communication increasingly depends on calibration, feedback and user control alongside decoding accuracy. Shared benchmarks enable algorithmic comparisons, while longitudinal studies reveal the demands of sustained use. | **[REST: Reliability-Guided Spatial Transcriptomics Supervision for Gene Expression Prediction from Histopathological HE-Stained Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26988v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Histopathology is central to cancer diagnosis, but morphology in Hematoxylin and Eosin (HE)-stained images does not directly reveal the molecular states that characterize disease heterogeneity and inform tumor characterization, prognostic assessment, and biomarker discovery. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics. Within this framework, we prove an impossibility result: no interpretation can simultaneously satisfy a collection of desirable properties while remaining non-circular. Each node in an EHR graph encodes feature identity, observed value, and temporal position of clinical events. A context-aware, time-decay message-passing mechanism models transitions between successive buckets, followed by multi-head attention pooling to generate patient-level representations. GraphBEAT was trained and evaluated on multi-site longitudinal diagnosis codes and laboratory tests to predict pancreatic, liver, and intestinal cancer at 12-, 24-, and 36-month diagnostic lead times. In all experimental setups, GraphBEAT consistently outperformed baseline graph neural networks (GraphSAGE, GAT, and GCN) and sequence modeling approaches (Transformer and long short-term memory [LSTM]). | **[Time to Fake It: Continual Machine Learning Intensifies Unfairness in Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21974v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deepfake detectors safeguard against misinformation, rendering their failures as much a societal as a technical concern. We find accuracy degradation to typically exacerbate unfairness and, conversely, fairness improvements to remain scarce over time. Dissecting the CL method families (rehearsal, prompts, regularization), we observe that inequitable forgetting is not explained by underrepresentation alone, but likely entangled with representation learning. We thus issue a call to action for CL methods to take social outcomes into account when balancing the stability-plasticity tradeoff.</p></details> |  |

| **[When to Align, When to Predict: A Phase Diagram for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11190v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-modal alignment (CA) and cross-modal prediction (CP) are the dominant paradigms for multimodal representation learning, yet there is no systematic understanding of when each succeeds and when each fails, a gap that leaves practitioners, especially in scientific domains with heterogeneous instruments and multiple levels of measurement, unable to diagnose why standard methods underperform the best single modality. | **[Monotone Piecewise-Linear Harmonization of Multitemporal Multispectral Surface Reflectance With Unsupervised Deep Multiview Pseudoinvariant Features Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21183v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Surface reflectance products derived from optical satellite may exhibit systematic inconsistencies across acquisition dates, because atmospheric correction is generally performed independently for each image. In particular, we show that models with a $p$-dimensional parameter can be identified from just $2p+1$ generic random Fourier features. ## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Discovering Causal Relationships Between Time Series With Spatial Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.26485v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal discovery is the subfield of causal inference concerned with estimating the structure of cause-and-effect relationships in a system of interrelated variables, as opposed to quantifying the strength or describing the form of causal effects. As interest in causal discovery builds in fields such as ecology, public health, and environmental sciences, where data are regularly collected with spatial and temporal structures, approaches must evolve to manage autocorrelation and complex confounding. As it stands, the few proposed causal discovery algorithms for spatiotemporal data require summarizing across locations, ignore spatial autocorrelation, and/or scale poorly to high dimensions. Here, we introduce our framework to extend time-series causal discovery to systems with spatial structure. Building upon work on causal discovery across contexts and methods for handling spatial confounding in causal effect estimation, we prove the soundness and completeness of our extension in the large sample limit. We close by discussing the feasibility of the framework, outlining remaining gaps in the literature, and identifying directions for future research.</p></details> | 12 pages, 2 figures |
| **[A Semi-Analytical Inference Framework for Structural Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28103v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structural Causal Models (SCMs) provide a principled framework for observational, interventional, and counterfactual reasoning, all of which rely on probabilistic inference. Existing frameworks either transform SCMs into alternative tractable representations or rely on approximate inference with expressive generative models. We instead perform inference using a message passing scheme directly on the SCM by representing structural mechanisms and probability densities in a common finite-dimensional function space. Forward propagation combines a numerical realization of the Law of the Unconscious Statistician, while propagated densities are recovered by spline projection from LOTUS moments. The same representation naturally yields backward message passing for conditioning, providing a unified framework for causal inference. Using tensor-product B-spline bases, the resulting algorithm expresses inference through numerical quadrature and linear algebra without explicitly constructing conditional distributions. Experiments on randomly generated continuous SCMs demonstrate accurate approximation of Monte Carlo reference solutions and systematic improvement with increasing basis resolution.</p></details> |  |
| **[Nonparametric Identification and Estimation of Causal Effects on Latent Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08681v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How can we conduct causal inference when the outcome of interest is latent and imperfectly measured by multiple indicators? We develop a nonparametric framework for identifying and estimating average treatment effects on latent outcomes in randomized experiments. Researchers commonly aggregate multiple outcome indicators into a summary measure before estimating treatment effects. We identify a fundamental but often overlooked comparability problem arising from this practice: adding, removing, or replacing indicators can alter the causal estimand. We formalize this problem and show that commonly used procedures generally fail to preserve comparability across measurement sets. To address this problem, we propose a design-based approach that uses nonparametric bridge functions. We further characterize the semiparametric efficiency bound, construct an efficient estimator, and establish necessary and sufficient conditions under which additional measurements yield strict efficiency gains. We illustrate our approach through an application to the Hajj visa lottery. Our framework provides practical guidance for measurement design, identification, and efficient causal estimation with latent outcomes.</p></details> |  |
| **[A plug-in estimator of the non-iterative conditional expectation g-formula using long short-term memory models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27354v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The non-iterative conditional expectation (NICE) plug-in estimator of the g-formula can be used to estimate the causal effects of sustained treatment strategies, even in the presence of treatment-confounder feedback, under the assumptions of exchangeability, positivity, and consistency. As an alternative to parametric models, long short-term memory (LSTM) models, designed to capture both short-term patterns and long-range dependencies in sequential data, may better accommodate complex temporal dependencies among time-varying confounders, treatments, and outcomes. However, the validity of statistical inferences of a plug-in estimator based on LSTM models has not yet been investigated. Here, we provide proof of principle that, under a complex data generation mechanism that resembles data from a cohort of persons with HIV and sufficiently large sample sizes, an LSTM-based NICE g-formula plug-in estimator results in less bias than parametric estimators while achieving near-nominal coverage for 95\% confidence intervals. This work is intended to spur the development of a theoretical framework that encompasses deep learning plug-in estimators for causal inference based on high-dimensional longitudinal data.</p></details> |  |
| **[More Treatments More Problems: Designing the 2026 American Causal Inference Conference Data Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26557v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper documents the design of the 2026 American Causal Inference Conference (ACIC) Data Challenge, which uses a large set of simulations to evaluate the performance of a wide variety of causal inference estimators chosen and implemented by challenge participants. Unlike previous ACIC Data Challenges, which tended to mimic observational studies with a single binary treatment, the 2026 Data Challenge focused on randomized control trials with five arms (one control and four active treatments) to isolate key estimation challenges that arise when simultaneously estimating multiple treatment effects. We created 9,000 distinct synthetic populations corresponding to 18 distinct settings to explore variation in performance corresponding to patterns of treatment effect heterogeneity and response surface specifications. From each population, a sample of n = 1,000 was drawn for participants to estimate individual, subgroup, sample, and population-level treatment effects and identify the optimal treatment arm at each level. We describe several strategies used to generate the data to prevent Challenge participants from exploiting structural sim across datasets and ``gaming the system.'' By detailing these simulation design considerations and data generation choices, this paper provides a framework for conducting large-scale, unconfounded synthetic benchmark studies to rigorously evaluate multi-treatment causal inference procedures.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where to weight? Estimating population causal effects with weighted double score matching in complex surveys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25870v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Double score matching is an emerging method for causal inference that combines propensity and prognostic scores to achieve dimension reduction and model robustness. We develop weighted double score matching (WDSM) that provides a unified framework for incorporating survey weights throughout the score estimation, matching, and outcome analysis under both retrospective (informative) and prospective (non-informative) sampling designs. We establish asymptotic normality of the resultant effect estimates under both known and estimated scores and develop a linearization-based bootstrap procedure for variance estimation that preserves the survey-weighted matching structure. Simulation studies show that WDSM substantially reduces bias relative to unweighted matching when either the propensity score model or the prognostic score models are correctly specified. Under correct specification, WDSM also improves efficiency and confidence interval coverage relative to survey-weighted propensity score matching, with particularly pronounced gains under poor overlap. Applications to early marijuana use and mental health in the National Survey on Drug Use and Health and to sleep duration and glycemic control in the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey illustrate the practical value of WDSM for population-level inference. We conclude with a discussion of our findings and provide practical guidance on incorporating survey weights into matching-based analyses of observational data.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 3 figures; 173-page Web Appendix included (198 pages total)</p></details> |
| **[Inverse Confounding Analysis: An Exact Method for Quantifying the Significance of Confounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11991v5)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The presence of unmeasured confounding factors during the collection of observational data may lead to biased estimates of the effect of an exposure on an outcome. Consequently, a central problem in causal inference based on observational data is sensitivity analysis with respect to unmeasured confounding. Existing sensitivity analyses generally focus on worst-case bounds. We propose an exact method for quantifying the significance of confounding, defined here in terms of the complete range of analytical estimates of the stratification-based Risk Ratio over the set of all joint distributions compatible with the observed characteristics. We refer to the proposed method as Inverse Confounding Analysis (ICA). Baseline results with a state-of-the-art LLM show that, while the model correctly identifies the high-level strategy in 81% of cases, full identification-specification correctness drops to only 38%, revealing that the bottleneck lies in the nuanced details of research design rather than in computation. CausalReasoningBenchmark is publicly available on Hugging Face and is designed to foster the development of more robust automated causal-inference systems.</p></details> |  |

The fourth and fifth bounds, the generalized assumption-free (GAF) and counterfactual assumption-free (CAF) bounds, utilize both the data and sensitivity parameters. ## LLM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Weight Placement Is Not Enough: Synchronization in Multi-Node CPU Decoding of Ternary LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28620v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ternary LLMs read only a few gigabytes per decoded token, yet on dual-socket servers with four NUMA nodes the fastest ternary runtime we found uses less than half of the memory bandwidth. Placing each weight on the node of the thread that reads it is necessary but not enough: in a decode engine with node-local weights that reuses ik_llama.cpp's kernels, threads on two generations of Xeon servers still spend 20-48% of each token waiting at synchronization points. Per-thread barrier traces show that most of the wait is for the slowest threads of each node; the barriers' own latency (microseconds each, but up to 12% of the token for the smallest model) and serial per-node work are smaller parts. On identical kernels, a Megatron-style column split beats a row-owned split without cross-node reductions in every configuration we measured, and isolating the number of barriers shows that most of its gain comes from its threads waiting less for each other and for their node leaders, and only a minority from having fewer barriers. Where decoding is DRAM-bound, the resulting engine is 1.5-2.1x faster than ik_llama.cpp with all cores and 1.5-1.9x faster than ik_llama.cpp at its fastest thread count (3.2x and 2.8x for a small model that runs from the L3 cache), at the same accuracy and with less energy per token.</p></details> | 15 pages, 9 figures |
| **[When Do LLMs Help with Feature Selection? We compare five classical baselines, frozen Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.3-70B selectors, and two GRPO-adapted Qwen2.5-7B models, reporting classification ROC-AUC and regression R^2 separately and using dataset-wise ranks for cross-task comparison. Across five seeded evaluation partitions, classical selectors generally achieve higher predictive performance than the tested 7B LLM/RL selectors, including under a pre-specified ~33% feature budget with strict train-only classical fitting, while the 7B selectors retain compact subsets. A cardinality-matched semantic sensitivity experiment shows that, for Zero-shot Qwen-7B, informative feature names improve regression performance over anonymized and shuffled alternatives (+0.1964 and +0.1606 mean R^2), whereas aggregate classification differences are smaller (+0.0159 and +0.0084 ROC-AUC) and dataset-dependent. These results characterize when semantic information benefits LLM-based feature selection and show that classical selectors remain strong baselines for predictive tabular feature selection.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 6 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[LLMs are Brittle to Simple Code Transformations: Introducing CETBench - A Benchmark for Code-Equivalence Checking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04019v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study how well LLMs can determine whether two programs are functionally equivalent. Each dataset instance is created by sampling a program pair and applying a random sequence of predefined code transformations, yielding either equivalent or non-equivalent pairs. To remedy this, we present a simple fine-tuning-based approach to boost LLM performance on the transformed pairs of programs. In our experiments, we perform ablations over the difficulty level of original programs, as well as the kind of transformations used in generating pairs for equivalence checking. Our analysis presents deep insights into the working of LLMs for the task of code-equivalence, and points to the fact that they may still be far from what could be termed as a semantic understanding of the underlying code.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in ACL Findings 2026</p></details> |

| **[When Dynamic Scaling Fails: A Scale-State Feedback Governs Recurrent-State Quantization in Linear-Attention and SSM LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15139v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Linear-attention and state-space LLMs carry long context in a fixed-size recurrent state, and quantizing that state is the natural way to cut its cost during long decode. Unlike a weight tensor, the state is rewritten at every step, so a quantizer applied at each step sits inside the recurrence and its errors compound. INT4 that leaves synthetic recall perfectly intact at the start destroys it a thousand steps later. The mechanism is a feedback between the quantizer scale and the state it is derived from. Yoking that scale to a full-precision reference removes the resulting norm explosion entirely, yet recovers only 1.8 of the 5.4 nats lost, so numerical instability and functional degradation are separable failures. The remainder is resolution-dependent, and in our per-step setting every outlier-aware scheme we test loses to plain persistent scaling. Allocating bits across layers does help, but under article-disjoint splits a learned allocation collapses onto the trivial rule of protecting the first few layers. Run out to 32K, group-wise INT4 is worth no more than discarding the state. Contain the scale rather than locate outliers, and report the decode horizon at which a bit-width was certified.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, 7 tables. NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Long-Context Foundation Models (non-archival). Extended version: adds a cross-architecture containment comparison on Mamba-2 and corrects two claims in the workshop version. v2 discusses three concurrent works and narrows two claims. Code and project page: https://avalon-s.github.io/dynamic-scaling-fails/</p></details> |

| **[After the Attacker Leaves: Benchmarking Carryover Attacks on LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28425v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM agents persist state across sessions via two channels: long-lived memory files silently prepended to the system prompt, and scheduled tasks waking the agent without user input. | **[Foreword: Pre-Generation Prediction and Characterisation of LLM Test Failures using the Jacobian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28187v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Testing LLM-based software is expensive: each input needs several sampled responses, each judged by a costly oracle. We present Foreword, a framework built on two ideas: learn from both passing and failing references, and represent inputs through the Jacobian lens (J-lens), an interpretability technique that reads intermediate activations out as scores over vocabulary tokens. Foreword pairs these representations with two estimators: a sparse logistic-regression probe or dual Gaussian Mixture Models (dual GMMs), which also yield a reference density (to flag inputs the references do not cover) and failing-class components (to group similar failures). On ten tasks and three open-weight LLMs, all four combinations of representations and estimators outperform Clotho on average, both for prediction of failure (ROC-AUC improves by 0.14 on average and by up to 0.31) and test prioritisation (15% more failures among the 100 top-ranked inputs on average, up to 33%). | **[Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. | **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. | **[BAIT: Can LLMs Find What Biases a Reader? | **[Ethical LLM-Assisted Research: A Framework for Responsible Delegation, Verification, and Epistemic Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23644v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are becoming routine instruments of scientific research, assisting with literature synthesis, hypothesis development, coding, and formal reasoning. | **[When Does Experience Memory Help Closed-Loop LLM Driving Agents? A Controlled Evaluation with Resampled Warmup Pools, Token-Matched Fillers, and Audited Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27714v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When does experience memory help a closed-loop LLM driving agent? Published gains cannot say, because the two factors we find to matter most, which warmup experiences the pool contains and what any injected tokens do to the prompt, are controlled in none of them. We answer with a controlled evaluation on one audited HighwayEnv testbed (1Hz, gpt-4o-mini for the decisive contrasts). We treat the warmup pool as a resampled random factor (ten disjoint pools) and add token-matched irrelevant-filler controls, oracle-certified diagnostics, caution-pricing metrics, and quantified audits of the baseline ports. The probe suite spans flat-text retrieval (a DiLu port), a single-frame grid port (RESPOND-inspired), our episodic temporal-graph memory, a retrieval-free consolidated rulebook, and a flat K-step observation-history baseline. Four findings follow, each a property of this testbed. (1) Headline memory gains belong to the warmup draw. The reported pool is the best of ten for both retrieval memories, which beat No-Memory in only one pool of ten (sign test p=0.011), and even the rulebook's apparent pool-immunity collapsed on five fresh draws. (2) No retrieval design tested exceeds token-matched filler. The collision-only merge ceiling is reached by a single 15-token caution instruction, and metrics that price caution erase the rulebook's lead. (3) Representational sophistication is nowhere robustly operative. (4) Baseline port quality silently inverts conclusions. On this testbed, a single-pool comparison without filler controls would have reported memory gains that these controls reverse. We release the testbed, audited ports, seeds, and caches.</p></details> | ACML 2026 |
| **[How Fine-Tuning Objectives Shape Layer-Wise Information in LLM Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27711v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Under matched conditions, classifier-head fine-tuning (\textsc{cls}) paradigm consistently outperforms the LLM-as-Judge sequence-to-sequence (\textsc{s2s}) on binary hallucination detection. We trace this gap to the representations induced by the two objectives. Static analysis shows that \textsc{cls} learns a task-specific discriminative direction, pushing hidden states into a clear two-cluster geometry with stronger class separation and linearly decodable label information. By contrast, \textsc{s2s} relies on the pretrained language-modeling head to make decisions. This minimizes the pressure to reorganize representations, leaving the hidden states much closer to the pretrained backbone. Our analysis also reveals that the \textsc{cls} signal saturates before the final layer, after which upper layers form a plateau of statistically interchangeable and largely redundant representations. However, endpoint redundancy alone does not show whether these layers are dispensable during training. Further training-dynamics analysis reveals 1) a lower pretrained substrate, 2) a narrow upper-middle \textbf{InfoWindow} where task information emerges, and 3) a later-synchronizing upper plateau that mirrors this signal. This mechanism motivates us to remove layers above the InfoWindow. Truncating 31\% of the network leaves accuracy unchanged within seed noise on Qwen3-4B and 8B and cuts latency by $1.4\times$. The same depth fraction, fixed before any analysis of the target models, transfers to Llama-3.2-3B and Mistral-7B-v0.3, and the layer structure behind it reappears there and on QNLI and SST-2. We hope this helps guide efficient task-specific LLM design.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, 6 figures, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Calibration: Temporal LLM Confidence Assessment Across Languages and Local Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27702v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are expected to estimate their own uncertainty, with confidence signals used to guide retrieval, adaptive compute allocation, routing, and selective abstention. Inspired by classical meta-memory theory, we distinguish anticipatory pre-solve confidence (Feeling-of-Knowing, FOK) from retrospective post-solve confidence (Judgement-of-Learning, JOL). We evaluate these signals across two frontier LLMs, multiple reasoning domains, translated Marathi benchmarks, and a newly constructed benchmark of genuinely local Marathi factual questions. Across settings, the cheaper pre-solve signal remains weakly calibrated, while post-solve confidence generally provides stronger epistemic alignment despite its additional inference cost. Confidence behaviour transfers surprisingly well under direct language translation, with calibration trends largely preserved between English and Marathi, but degrades substantially on local Marathi knowledge, suggesting that linguistic transfer alone is insufficient for region-specific information needs. Finally, hallucinated responses maintain persistently elevated confidence after answer generation, highlighting important limitations of current LLM self-assessment mechanisms.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 7 figures, WiNLP @ EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking LLM Fine-Tuning via Weight Space Reparameterization: Preserving Safety during Downstream Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26443v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on downstream tasks often weakens their previously aligned safety behavior, creating an inherent trade-off between task adaptation and safety preservation. Existing approaches attempt to mitigate this issue by identifying safety-relevant neurons, layers, or update directions in the original parameter space. However, safety-related information is often distributed across many directions in this space, causing downstream updates to overwrite parameters that encode safety behavior. We propose WSR-Tune, a Weight Space Reparameterization-based fine-tuning framework that preserves safety during downstream adaptation. Our approach constructs a safety-conditioned basis and reparameterizes the weight matrices such that safety-relevant information becomes concentrated in a small subset of directions. We then identify safety-critical directions and freeze them in the reparameterized space, while updating the remaining complementary directions for downstream adaptation. By explicitly structuring the parameter space to disentangle safety and task information, our method reduces interference between objectives and enables simultaneous preservation of safety and improvement in downstream performance. Experimental results show that our method achieves a more favorable trade-off between downstream performance and safety retention, demonstrating its effectiveness for reliable LLM fine-tuning.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 5 figures. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026(spotlight)</p></details> |
| **[Blackbox Performance Analysis of LLM-based Network Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24348v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs are becoming an indispensable part of network control systems due to their impressive reasoning. However, auditing these systems is challenging due to the inherent opacity of LLMs. LENS is the first step towards black-box performance analysis of LLM-enabled network control systems. It uses novel methods to extend deep reinforcement learning to guide the search for performance gaps and inputs that make LLMs struggle. LENS finds large gaps for LLM-based traffic engineering and GPU allocation systems, and even matches existing white-box analyzers designed for hand-crafted TE heuristics.</p></details> |  |
| **[PersonaFactor: Preference Factors with Dynamic Selection for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16372v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although users may express different personalization needs across tasks, these task-specific behaviors can reflect shared underlying preference mechanisms. We construct a library of thirteen PersonaFactors by extracting evidence-grounded rationales and clustering recurring patterns across tasks, and develop a dynamic factor-guided generation framework that selects factors relevant to each user's history and current request. Across four personalization benchmarks and four backbone models, Dynamic PersonaFactor achieves the best score on every reported metric. Representation analyses further show that PersonaFactors and user history induce partially overlapping low-dimensional representations, and that their joint use yields complementary shifts. Taken together, these findings suggest that factor-based and history-based personalization can mutually reinforce each other.</p></details> |  |
| **[CHIMERA: Compact Synthetic Data for Generalizable LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00889v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have exhibited remarkable reasoning capabilities, largely enabled by supervised fine-tuning (SFT)- and reinforcement learning (RL)-based post-training on high-quality reasoning data. However, reproducing and extending these capabilities in open and scalable settings is hindered by three data-centric challenges: (1) the cold-start problem, arising from the lack of seed datasets with detailed, long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) trajectories needed to initialize reasoning policies; (2) limited domain coverage, as most existing open-source reasoning datasets are concentrated in mathematics, with limited coverage of broader scientific disciplines; and (3) the annotation bottleneck, where the difficulty of frontier-level reasoning tasks makes reliable human annotation prohibitively expensive or infeasible. To address these challenges, we introduce CHIMERA, a compact synthetic reasoning dataset of 9K samples designed to support generalizable reasoning across domains. CHIMERA is constructed with three key properties: (1) it provides rich, long CoT reasoning trajectories synthesized by state-of-the-art reasoning models; (2) it has broad and structured coverage, spanning 8 major scientific disciplines and over 1K fine-grained topics organized via a model-generated hierarchical taxonomy; and (3) it employs a fully automated, scalable evaluation pipeline that uses strong reasoning models to cross-validate both problem validity and answer correctness, removing the reliance on human annotations. We use CHIMERA to post-train a 4B Qwen3 model with SFT and RL. Despite the dataset's modest size, the resulting model achieves strong performance on a suite of challenging reasoning benchmarks, including GPQA-Diamond, AIME 24/25/26, HMMT 25 and Humanity's Last Exam, approaching or matching the performance of substantially larger models such as DeepSeek-R1 and Qwen3-235B.</p></details> |  |
| **[What a One-Hour Interview Does Not Tell an LLM About You: Interviews Elicit Traits, Not Decision Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29458v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly asked to stand in for particular people, and interviewing the person is the natural way to tell a model who they are. We test what a one-hour LLM-led adaptive interview tells a model about an individual's value-laden decisions. 46 adults completed such an interview and then answered 25 dilemma questions, which are the ground truth. We predict their answers from different parts of the transcript, compare adaptive follow-up questions with fixed ones on the same participants, and benchmark every variant against a model given no information about the person and against the majority answer. No part of the interview is reliably better than either baseline, and adaptive follow-ups are no better than fixed ones. Two audits of the transcripts show why. For 85% of the errors an auditor finds no evidence that distinguishes the person's choice from the alternatives, and where a transcript does contain related evidence, the option it favours matches the person's own answer less often than the majority answer does. The same transcripts do improve recovery of the person's MBTI type. Interviews of this kind elicit traits, not decision policies. We discuss what decision-aware elicitation would require and why persona agents should not be delegated value-laden decisions, and we release the consenting participants' transcripts with all predictions.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 5 figures, 27 tables. Substantially revised version: new title, a second cohort (N=46), a within-subject fixed follow-up control, zero-context and majority baselines, and two transcript audits</p></details> |
| **[Local Allow, Global Deny: Why Per-Action Authorization Does Not Compose in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27620v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous LLM agents act through tools under per-action authorization: a local guard screens each tool call in isolation. We show that local authorization does not compose into global authorization. A compositional authorization failure (CAF) is a locally authorized execution, or a pair of locally authorized executions, that violates a global policy spanning intent, purpose, provenance, consent, and delegation. We characterize CAFs along two axes, expressiveness (single-trace safety versus relational k-safety) and observability (a ladder of monitor profiles). Our central result is an impossibility: relational purpose violations form a 2-safety hyperproperty with no per-trace locus of blame, so a per-action monitor that never raises a false alarm detects none of them, independent of the underlying model or decision procedure; they become visible only under cross-execution monitoring, which none of the six guardrails we survey implements. We make this constructive with AL-SYNTH, a SAT/SMT synthesizer (using no language model) that emits minimal CAF witnesses, each checked against an exhaustive oracle. The empirical signature is sharp: across nine LLM judges the per-action view approves the synthesized witness almost without exception, blocking it at a rate comparable to a benign control, yet the same models flag it when shown the two executions together. Per-action authorization is therefore structurally insufficient for relational policy conjuncts not reducible to single-trace safety: to bound them, a monitor must compare executions or accept false alarms.</p></details> | <details><summary>Exten...</summary><p>Extended version of a paper accepted at the 8th IEEE International Conference on Trust, Privacy and Security in Intelligent Systems and Applications (IEEE TPS 2026), with full proofs and appendices. 12 pages, 5 figures, 7 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/anmolpandey299/al-synth</p></details> |
| **[When LLMs Manipulate: Measuring Behavioral, Social, and Temporal Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27573v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can influence user trust and decision-making through subtle linguistic cues, yet the behavioral effects of manipulative outputs remain poorly quantified. We introduce a behavioral computing framework for measuring \textit{behavioral vulnerability}, defined as users' susceptibility to manipulative AI responses. Building on DarkPatterns-LLM benchmark, we generate controlled manipulative stimuli using Manipulation Gradient Descent (MGD), targeting biases associated with authority, urgency, and social proof. We analyze participant responses using the Multi-Stakeholder Impact Assessment Network (MSIAN) to model cross-user influence and the Temporal Harm Propagation Model (THPM) to characterize the persistence of behavioral effects over time. In a study involving 120 participants, manipulative framing produced significant changes in trust calibration and compliance ($p < 0.01$), with effects also propagating across social contexts. These findings highlight the need to extend LLM safety evaluation beyond model outputs to measurable behavioral consequences and provide a computational framework for quantifying individual, social, and temporal risks arising from manipulative AI interactions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Response-Informed Value Estimation for Cost-Aware Multi-Turn LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23239v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM routing selects a model at every turn before its response is available, yet task success is revealed only when the interaction ends, leaving each intermediate choice without a label of its own. Logged trajectories, however, retain the response each call produced, which exposes what each turn required and how well the chosen model served it, evidence that outcomes alone hide. We introduce RIVET (Response-Informed Value Estimation for Turn-Level Routing), which exploits this post-call evidence during training while keeping routing strictly pre-call at deployment. RIVET jointly trains two value estimators over a shared state-model representation: a deployable Routing Value Estimator that scores candidate models from pre-call information, and a training-only Response-Informed Value Estimator that additionally observes the realized response. Conditioning on the response turns response-induced return variation from residual noise into an observed covariate, and we show that this reduces the leading asymptotic estimation error of the shared representation. On the MINT benchmark, RIVET solves more held-out tasks than the strongest multi-turn baseline while using 74.7% less total call cost.</p></details> |  |
| **[Service-Induced Congestion in Memory-Constrained LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15555v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In large language model (LLM) serving, each request accumulates persistent graphics processing unit (GPU) memory during service as its key-value cache grows with every generated token. Under high concurrency, aggregate memory usage therefore increases endogenously over time: the service process itself creates future capacity pressure. When memory capacity is exceeded, systems evict active requests, discarding cached state and restarting them later, which wastes computation and reduces throughput. We develop a discrete-time dynamical model of memory-constrained LLM inference that captures admission, memory growth, and eviction under continuous batching. In the saturated-input regime, the system admits both eviction-free fixed points and limit cycles with evictions. For homogeneous workloads, we show that the eviction-free equilibrium is unstable and that, except for a Lebesgue-measure-zero exact-capture set, the system converges to a unique worst-case limit cycle that is asymptotically stable outside this exceptional set, with throughput losses as large as 50%. For heterogeneous workloads, we prove a stability criterion in the two-class common-input setting and explain how the survival-polynomial mechanism generalizes to multiple classes and heterogeneous-input lengths. Under an input-dominated scaling regime, coprime decoding lengths stabilize the eviction-free equilibrium, while non-coprime lengths create synchronized modes that drive instability. These results characterize when workload heterogeneity desynchronizes completions and helps stabilize memory-constrained serving. More broadly, we identify service-induced congestion as a structural instability mechanism and derive scheduling design principles for sustaining high throughput.</p></details> | <details><summary>100 p...</summary><p>100 pages. v2: adds GPU experiments with benchmark workloads (Appendix D.4) and revises the figures</p></details> |
| **[Act on Your Behalf, Beyond Your Authorization: Benchmarking LLM Agent Authorization Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27548v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM agent acts on behalf of a user, it is expected to act within the authorization scope the user grants. Existing agent evaluations largely measure task completion or harmful consequences and can therefore overlook actions that violate this delegation without producing conventional safety or privacy harms. We introduce AuthorizationBench, a benchmark of authorization alignment grounded in documented real-world cases. A scalable construction pipeline produces 286 executable tasks spanning situational triggers, the presence or absence of direct harmful consequences, and ten application domains. We execute every task with four models at two reasoning-effort settings across three agent harnesses in an authorization-aware sandbox that keeps authorized and out-of-authorization paths executable and observable, yielding 6,864 trajectories. Agents act out of authorization in 19.2% of trajectories, with rates ranging from 10.5% to 26.6% across the 24 configurations. Task completion does not imply authorization. Increasing reasoning effort leaves the aggregate rate nearly unchanged, with effects varying across models. On controlled pairs, agents act out of authorization at similar rates with (17.9%) and without (20.8%) direct harmful consequences, and more often under benign pressures than malicious injections (23.5% versus 17.6%, p=0.002). Our results show that task capability, additional reasoning, and consequence-oriented safety evaluation do not by themselves ensure authorization alignment. Moreover, agents that consult the principal before completing a task exhibit lower rates of out-of-authorization behavior (14.0% versus 21.6%, p<0.001), pointing to a tension between agent autonomy and preserving users' decision authority that calls for further human-centered study. Dataset and code are available at https://github.com/PEACH-Research-Lab/AuthorizationBench .</p></details> |  |
| **[VeriRouter: Towards Verifiable LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17431v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) routers are increasingly deployed to optimize quality, latency, and cost through dynamic model selection and request rewriting. However, a compromised router can cause ineligible selection on the control plane, unendorsed rewriting or unfaithful relay on the data plane, undermining LLM routing integrity. We present VeriRouter, a verifiable LLM routing framework. Under an endorsed public context binding a private semantic index and rewrite banks, VeriRouter combines ZK proofs with a provider-issued serving certificate to establish integrity across both planes while keeping the router's private routing assets hidden from clients. For model selection, we design an efficient ZK proof for semantic search. For rewriting, local checks enforce message preservation and additions from committed banks. On real-world workloads, VeriRouter's model-selection and rewrite proofs average 21.92~s and 4.24~s, yielding 1,307$\times$ and 101$\times$ speedups over the fastest completing baselines. Relative to serving without verification, VeriRouter adds only 4.08 s under per-turn selection and 0.11 s under session-pinned selection.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Language Learners Code-Switch: Korean-English Code-Switching in LLM-based Speaking Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23136v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code-switching (CSW), the use of multiple languages within the same interaction, has been widely studied in NLP for multilingual communication. While CSW also occurs in language-learning contexts, the functions and pedagogical implications of learner CSW in learner-AI interactions remain underexplored. To address this gap, we introduce LAKE, a dataset of Learner-AI dialogues with Korean-English CSW. This dataset captures South Korean learners' CSW behavior during English speaking practice with LLM-based conversation partners, comprising 675 dialogues, 19.6K utterances, and 43.9 hours of learner speech. We develop a taxonomy of learner CSW functions, finding that learners switch to Korean for diverse purposes, including signaling lexical gaps, negotiating meaning, and conveying culturally specific content. We also observe learners reusing and adapting English expressions introduced by the LLM partner following their code-switches, suggesting opportunities for contextualized lexical support. To support research on speech recognition for learner CSW, we release 4.9 hours of code-switched learner speech with human-verified transcripts and benchmark speech-to-text models on Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), revealing difficulties in recognizing learner CSW. These findings highlight learner CSW as a communicative and pedagogically meaningful resource that can inform the design of AI-based conversational language learning systems.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[Difference-in-Differences on a Censored Rating Scale Can Manufacture an Effect: Evidence from a Pre-Registered LLM-Judge Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27309v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-judge audits assess bias by comparing ratings across matched conditions. Difference-in-differences designs compare two candidate responses within each item and then compare that contrast across a manipulated attribute, with an endpoint read off a bounded rating scale. We examine this problem in a pre-registered audit comprising 990 calls to a frozen pedagogy judge. The registered primary analysis did not detect an effect of a stated novice or advanced profile on scaffolding preference. The only nominally significant secondary interaction concerned the productive-struggle sub-score ($+0.378$ points; $p = 0.002$). A post-hoc construction with zero differential preference reproduced 79 to 85% of this interaction using the observed severity shift and the scale floor alone. The observed interaction therefore does not identify differential preference on the latent scale.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NewInML Workshop at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Scalar Leaderboards: Adaptive Sampling for Pareto Frontier Identification in Multi-Objective LLM Arenas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27447v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The evaluation of large language models (LLMs) is inherently multi-objective, as different models may offer different tradeoffs across performance dimensions such as math, code, and writing. Because such evaluations are often based on costly and time-consuming human feedback, it is important to draw reliable conclusions using as few comparisons as possible. In this paper, we present Vector-Borda Empirical-Gap Elimination (VB-EGE), a fixed-confidence adaptive sampling algorithm for efficiently identifying models on the Pareto frontier in arena-style evaluation, where each comparison evaluates a pair of models on a prompt with respect to a selected performance dimension. VB-EGE progressively eliminates models from an active set once sufficient statistical evidence has been accumulated to certify their Pareto status. Under a multi-objective Bradley-Terry model, we show that VB-EGE returns the correct Pareto frontier with high probability and derive an instance-dependent upper bound on its sample complexity. We further establish a worst-case lower bound showing that VB-EGE is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors. Simulations and experiments on three real-world Arena datasets (Arena, SearchArena, and VisionArena) demonstrate VB-EGE's empirical effectiveness.</p></details> | <details><summary>51 pa...</summary><p>51 pages, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[More Memory, Worse Agents: Error Reproduction and Anti-Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27406v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. A central design question is whether to retain only observed trajectories and outcomes or also the rules, summaries, and skills inferred from them. Retaining these abstractions allows agents to reuse strategies without repeatedly deriving them from raw trajectories. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. These results support separating experience accumulation from abstraction persistence in memorybased self-improvement.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures, 7 tables, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[The Machine's Internal Clock: Do LLMs Share Human Temporal Illusions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15394v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human perception of time is subjective. Temporal illusions show that the brain relies on context and relational cues when judging duration. Prior studies established these effects with visual and auditory stimuli. We ask whether written narratives alone can cause these illusions, and whether LLMs respond as human participants do. We introduce a benchmark of 6,684 paired scenarios spanning five illusions, each judged from the character's and the reader's perspective. In our human study (60 participants), participants' judgments matched the predicted directions for all five illusions in at least one perspective (7 of 10 conditions). Across 14 LLMs, models reproduce every effect humans show in the same direction, but on average more strongly. Most effects persisted even after cue words were removed and after controlling for 15 lexical and semantic features. These results show that models and humans agree in direction but not in strength, which cautions against treating LLM judgments as substitutes for human responses for subjective temporal reasoning.</p></details> | 27 pages, 19 figures |
| **[LLM-Attack Suite: A Unified Red-Team Framework for Privacy Evaluation of Split and Distributed LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22722v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Split language models keep input text on a client but send intermediate representations to a server. Attacks can reconstruct text from these messages. Comparing privacy evaluations is difficult when attackers see different messages, know different model weights or use different datasets and recovery measures. We present LLM-Attack Suite, a framework that records these choices and lets researchers check, compare and extend an evaluation. Compatible attacks reuse recorded messages, and their predictions are saved for independent scoring. The scoring rule requires a linked undefended reference before a defence can pass. Reports also identify attacks that remain untested. In a controlled GPT-2 experiment, adding gradients returned during training increased token recovery from 94.20% to 97.38% and exact-document recovery from 13.71% to 37.77%. The 95% interval for the token gain lay above the two-percentage-point threshold fixed before testing. This experiment is shared with a companion study; here it tests how the evaluation responds to a change in attacker access. Further studies measure how corpus choice and compression defences affect recovery, compare an adapted published attack with its reference implementation, and test the measurement code. The framework reports implementation checks separately from measured attack effectiveness. Each privacy claim should state the attacker, dataset and scoring rule, report its controls and identify untested attacks.</p></details> |  |
| **[WhatIf: Interactive Exploration of Large-Scale LLM Agent Simulations for Policy Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17615v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Policymakers in domains such as emergency management, public health, and urban planning must make decisions under deep uncertainty, where outcomes depend on how large populations interpret information, coordinate, and adopt over time. Existing tools only partially support this process: tabletop exercises enable collaborative discussion but lack dynamic feedback, while computational simulations capture population dynamics but are designed for offline analysis. We present WhatIf, an interactive system that enables policymakers to steer, inspect, and compare LLM-powered social simulations in real time. Informed by a formative study in emergency preparedness planning, we derive four design requirements for interactive policy simulations: fluid steering, real-time scale, collaborative exploration, and multi-level interpretability. We developed WhatIf guided by these requirements and evaluated it with five preparedness professionals across three disaster evacuation scenarios. Our findings show that participants used the system as a space for iterative branching and comparison rather than evaluating fixed plans; reflected on tacit planning assumptions when agent behavior violated expectations; surfaced previously unrecognized planning vulnerabilities; and grounded their reasoning in inspectable agent-level cases rather than aggregate outputs alone. These findings suggest broader design implications for LLM-powered social simulation systems: designing such systems as interactive, shared reasoning environments--rather than offline predictive tools--can better support expert decision-making under deep uncertainty.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ACM Symposium on User Interface Software and Technology (UIST)</p></details> |
| **[On the LLM Verification for Argument Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27361v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems can formalize natural-language argumentation end-to-end, turning plain text into structured graphs by classifying components, connecting them with relations, and enriching the resulting graphs into formal models. Quality assurance for those graphs is almost always another LLMs: a reviewer, judge, or critic agent. On the other side, in adjacent neuro-symbolic fields, such generated output is often verified symbolically by external checks and solvers. In this work, we argue that in argument graph verification, the allocation should be similar, and based on what each instrument can guarantee. We propose layered verification: starting from deterministic checks, moving to solver-based and LLM reviews, and utilizing debate only under uncertainty, leaving human judgment for what no other instrument can decide. We ground our principle with a study on human-annotated graphs with injected structural defects. An LLM reviewer detects them at near-ceiling recall, including cycles in the support relation, but it objects to almost one in a ten correct graphs, even though it should not, happens to be inconsistent in verdict on identical inputs and is significantly slower than the equivalent exact check. It shows that while LLM reviewer does not lack the ability to detect structural defects, it is worth having a deterministic verification gates.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for GaLM workshop at CIKM 2026</p></details> |

When facts change and each memory is judged only by its own verified outcomes, one number, the certification load, sets how much reasoning any safe memory can skip, and under Zipf demand it yields a scaling law. During training, \method{} completes the task twice from each selected state, once with a short reasoning allowance and once with a long allowance, and compares their final quality and total processing to estimate when longer reasoning is valuable. During inference, a controller weighs this estimate against learned prices on remaining-budget pressure and chooses either short or long reasoning without running both alternatives. We evaluate PORA across application, memory, code, retail, and mathematics tasks, achieving strong results across all settings. | **[PROVISO: Provenance-Informed Action Selection for Secure LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27115v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents act on the external world through tools and third-party skills, so a single executed action can cause irreversible harm. | **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. | **[How Do Agentic LLMs Decide to Call Tools? Behavioral ablations show that the scaffold establishes a tool-call prior; Transcoder decomposition then reveals that analysis verbs suppress this prior through features signaling that tool use is unnecessary, whereas execution verbs largely leave it intact. To bridge this gap, we conduct a joint hardware-software (HW-SW) co-design exploration of the above underexplored MX-like design space with three innovations. 1) We define and systematically characterize the full 8-bit scale format family within the MX-like design space, jointly with asymmetric weight-activation precision, identifying configurations that outperform the MX and NVFP4-like (without per-tensor scaling, PTS) baselines. 2) We build a per-format specialized MAC datapath template that enables fair hardware cost evaluation across the space. 3) We develop an automated co-design flow, integrated into the open-source SNAX NPU platform, that enables joint HW-SW analysis across the design space. Overall, our co-design exploration reveals that scale format matters only under aggressive FP4 quantization, yielding a case-oriented design guideline for both scale format and asymmetric weight-activation precision. We further identify UE5M3 as a favorable accuracy-hardware tradeoff for the FP4 regime, reducing perplexity degradation by up to 25.3% relative to the NVFP4-like baseline, at a cost of only +0.06% area and +0.49% power overhead.</p></details> |  |

| **[GBClaw: An agentic AI framework for atomistic grain boundary research and mechanistic discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28629v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) systems hold immense potential for automating complex workflows in materials discovery and design, but extending them to domain-intensive mechanistic investigations remains challenging. Here, we present GBClaw, an agentic AI framework for investigating the structure and deformation of general grain boundaries (GBs). Although general GBs constitute the vast majority of real-world interfaces in polycrystalline materials, they remain understudied because their structural complexity and interfacial mismatch complicate experimental and modeling research. GBClaw integrates large language models (LLMs) into a modular architecture comprising an orchestrator, specialized agents, domain-specific tools, simulation interfaces, and hierarchical memory. Through dynamic replanning and iterative workflow refinement, the framework effectively connects systematic simulations with targeted mechanistic analysis. Applied to 14 general asymmetric tilt GBs in nickel, GBClaw characterized interfacial defect structures and identified diverse deformation modes across these incommensurate boundaries. Comparing boundary sliding with and without secondary GB misfit dislocations revealed substantial differences in shear strength, underscoring the critical role of defect hierarchies in sliding resistance. Furthermore, researcher-guided follow-up calculations traced an anomalously high shear resistance to metastable boundary structures. This work demonstrates that combining agentic AI with domain expertise bridges high-throughput computational screening with deep physical understanding of complex material interfaces.</p></details> |  |
| **[TraceLite: Efficient Trace Reduction for SWE Agents via Optical Compression and Redundancy Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28612v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software engineering agents solve repository-level tasks through iterative tool use, but their continuously growing interaction traces incur substantial token consumption and inference overhead. Existing compression approaches often require model training, auxiliary LLM calls, or assumptions about specific content such as source code. We present TraceLite, a simple yet effective, training-free framework for compressing heterogeneous SWE-agent observations. Our preliminary study shows that optical rendering preserves substantial textual and semantic information under moderate compression, while agent trajectories exhibit both cross-step and phase-level redundancy. Motivated by these findings, TraceLite preserves recent observations as high-fidelity text, renders historical observations as compact images, removes overlapping content, and discards phase-expired observation payloads after code editing. It requires neither offline training nor auxiliary LLM inference. We evaluate TraceLite on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE using GPT-5.6-Luna and Gemini-3.8Flash as backbone models. On SWE-bench Verified, TraceLite achieves 78.25% resolve rate, outperforming the evaluated compression baselines while reducing average input-token consumption and API cost by 33.05% and 21.30%, respectively. Moreover, it introduces only 0.438 seconds of compression latency per observation. These results demonstrate that TraceLite improves SWE-agent efficiency with low processing overhead and without compromising issue-resolution capability. Our code is available at https://github.com/xjywhu/TraceLite.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evolving Agent Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28601v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents are limited by the interfaces humans give them. We argue that interface design should itself be an agent capability. We introduce Agent S4, an autonomous computer-use scientist that builds Evolving Agent Interfaces from task experience. A meta-agent examines task agents' trajectories on synthetic training tasks, without success labels, and constructs and revises interfaces and usage knowledge in a repository. Population-based evolution then selects which repositories to develop further by their performance on validation tasks the meta-agent never sees, favoring changes that generalize beyond the seen tasks. On OSWorld V2, Agent S4, with Qwen3.8-27B and the evolved repository of interfaces, sets a new state of the art in open-source computer-use agents, reaching 21.3% binary score, 3.8 times the same model with fixed interfaces. This is above both GPT-5.5 (13.0%) and Claude Opus 4.8 (20.6%), at about 4.3% and 1.5% of their respective costs. On ScienceBoard, the evolved interfaces improve the success rate from 41.4% to 58.6% with the same model. These results suggest that useful interfaces can be evolved from experience, making autonomous interface design a source of improvement complementary to advances in models and harnesses. | **[AgenticRoboRL: Bringing Coding-Agent Post-Training to Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28589v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic robotics systems combine planners with robot harnesses to perform complex manipulation tasks. To this end, we introduce AgenticRoboRL, a framework for post-training robot planners across different harnesses. | **[Capable Teacher, Myopic Scorer: Reward-Free Teacher Repair for Agentic On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28586v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic on-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student agent on its own trajectories while a teacher scores every token. A natural explanation of where it goes wrong is compounding error: the student's mistakes carry its trajectories beyond the region where the teacher is reliable, so recent methods keep training inside that region, through episode-length curricula, teacher-guided turns, prefix replay, or teacher intervention where the two disagree. We show this explanation is incomplete. From the states where the student departs most from the teacher, the teacher's own takeovers still finish the task, yet the student trained on its token scores keeps running out of turns. We attribute this to the myopia of next-token scoring. The teacher rates each token without seeing where the episode ends, so its scores let through the safe, delaying moves that outcome-based RL would punish with zero reward, a penalty agentic OPD lacks. We call this difference between what the teacher does and what it scores the rollout-scorer gap. We propose Rollout-to-Scorer Distillation (R2S), which repairs the scorer before distillation by mining high-disagreement states from the untrained student, running teacher takeovers from them, keeping the states where enough takeovers pass a reward-free check such as consensus or an LLM judge, and fine-tuning the teacher to give its takeover-informed scores on every assistant token from the divergence turn onward, without the takeover in context. The repaired teacher is frozen as the sole scorer for standard OPD, requiring no task reward during repair or training. Across three environments, two teachers and four students, R2S outperforms three state-of-the-art agentic-OPD methods in all twelve teacher-student-environment cells, by 12.8 success points on average over the best of them in each cell. Project page: https://tree-shu-zhao.github.io/r2s/.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures, 13 tables. Project page: https://tree-shu-zhao.github.io/r2s/</p></details> |

| **[Cache Engineering for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28577v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at https://github.com/thu-nics/AKV.</p></details> |  |

| **[Proof-Carrying Contracts: Commitments AI Agents Cannot Break](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28484v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents increasingly act on behalf of people or other agents, purchasing services, exchanging information and delegating work.
